TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* AI Made the List—but Humans Still Did the Killing
  Foreign Policy | 05 Oct 21:08 UTC
  In the film “NAZA,” Israeli soldiers reveal the workings of autonomous warfare.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. For those of us growing weary of the predictions made by Silicon Valley CEOs about artificial intelligence possibly leading to the extermination of the human race, the new Israeli documentary NAZA is a counterpoint, even if that wasn’t the filmmakers’ intention. The nearly 80-minute film takes us far away from the tiresome tech doomers (who keep racing ahead with the technology despite their apocalyptic concerns ) and transports us to the nighttime rooftops of Tel Aviv, where Israeli filmmakers Yuval Abraham and Rachel Szor interview 24 anonymous members of the Israel Defense Forces (IDF) about their role in the relentless military strikes on Gaza over the past three years. For those of us growing weary of the predictions made by Silicon Valley CEOs about artificial intelligence possibly leading to the extermination of the human race, the new Israeli documentary NAZA is a counterpoint, even if that wasn’t the filmmakers’ intention. The nearly 80-minute film takes us far away from the tiresome tech doomers (who keep racing ahead with the technology despite their apocalyptic concerns ) and transports us to the nighttime rooftops of Tel Aviv, where Israeli filmmakers Yuval Abraham and Rachel Szor interview 24 anonymous members of the Israel Defense Forces (IDF) about their role in the relentless military strikes on Gaza over the past three years. NAZA , which takes its name from the Hebrew acronym for “collateral damage,” focuses heavily on the AI systems that became an integral part of the war in Gaza after the Hamas attack of Oct. 7, 2023. “‘You have to trust the system,’” one soldier recalls an officer telling him. Otherwise, “‘everything will fall apart.’” The soldiers talk about the role they played in Israeli programs such as Lavender, an AI system that identifies enemy targets, and the unfortunately named Where’s Daddy, which involves using children’s cellphones to track when a Hamas target—their father—returns home. In the case of Where’s Daddy, the resulting strikes, as one soldier recounts, would kill the suspected Hamas member but typically also his family, including the child whose phone was hacked. Some of the concerns expressed by soldiers in NAZA are not new, such as modern warfare being akin to a video game, a sentiment frequently expressed since the advent of armed drones and push-button warfare.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/05/israel-gaza-palestine-ai-war-naza-civilians/

  FULL TEXT: “The Republic is secular; France is Christian”—a phrase that sounds even more definitive in French: “ La République est laïque, la France est chrétienne ”—is frequently attributed to Charles de Gaulle, the very Catholic founder of the Fifth Republic. It is very much a phrase that can also be attributed to the French in 2026 as well—that is, if Pope Leo XIV’s visit to Paris is any indication. In addition, there were the extreme-right National Rally’s Marine Le Pen and Jordan Bardella, neither of whom have ever served in government. * Russia’s Air War Aims to Bleed Ukraine Dry
  Foreign Policy | 05 Oct 14:51 UTC
  Vladimir Putin’s latest plan for victory puts a cease-fire well out of reach.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Understanding the conflict four years on. Marking a new phase after almost five years of war, Russia is intensifying and widening its air war against Ukraine. Now that Ukrainian forces have stopped and partially reversed the Russian battlefield advance, the Kremlin appears to have embraced a new theory of victory. By not only increasing the number of missile and drone strikes but expanding the range of their targets, Russian President Vladimir Putin hopes to paralyze Ukraine’s economy, society, and basic functioning of the state as winter approaches. In the past few months, Russia has become more systematic, deliberate, and precise in striking civilian infrastructure. On the back of increased production of ballistic missiles and newer, faster drones, the Kremlin has widened the air campaign to target Ukrainian railways, gas stations, port infrastructure, cargo ships, shopping malls, and data centers . Russia’s plan also includes cutting Ukrainian cities off from electricity, heating, and water . On the back of increased production of ballistic missiles and newer, faster drones, the Kremlin has widened the air campaign to target Ukrainian railways, gas stations, port infrastructure, cargo ships, shopping malls, and data centers . Russia’s plan also includes cutting Ukrainian cities off from electricity, heating, and water . This campaign is not merely Russia ramping up attacks against civilians and objects in the rear. It is an attempt to strangle Ukraine’s economy, disrupt supply chains, cut transport routes, and destroy public utilities. By systematically targeting the infrastructure that allows Ukraine’s society and economy to function, Russia aims to make Ukraine uninhabitable as winter approaches. * How the U.S. Can Once Again Lead on Human Rights

  In the post-Trump era, Washington will need to regain credibility. Pluralism is where it should start.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. For 80 years, the United States styled itself as a defender of human rights and freedom on the global stage. While its record was never pure, the language of values cut across Republican and Democratic administrations, lending credibility and distinctiveness to Washington’s global leadership. Even routine charges of hypocrisy underscored that there were certain principles that Washington purported to stand for, though they might too often be honored in the breach. Of the many pillars of U.S. foreign policy that President Donald Trump’s administration has sent tumbling down, values-based leadership may be among the toughest to erect anew. That’s because it is premised on firm ideals and commitments. While U.S. partners have come to expect a certain amount of wavering, fundamental betrayals are hard to overcome. By the end of Trump’s second term—12 years after he first came to power—younger generations will have little lived memory of values-based U.S. leadership. Already, when surveyed, people under 35 in traditional U.S. allied countries are especially likely to say U.S. democracy has never been a model worth following.   A Hebrew term for complacent blindness still captures Netanyahu’s policies.
  FULL TEXT: On Oct. 7, 2023, thousands of Hamas attackers breached Israel’s layered southern barriers around Gaza, killing around 1,200 people and taking 251 hostages. Israel’s military, intelligence, and political systems systematically failed, making the surprise attack possible and then allowing Hamas to run roughshod for nearly three days . These failures are the subject of several recent books and articles , both in Hebrew and English. Collectively, they show that Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his top defense officials were almost unfathomably confident in the status quo, ignoring clear warnings that Hamas was preparing a major attack. Some have turned to conspiracy theories to explain this, claiming that Netanyahu deliberately permitted the attack to happen. The rational, and at least partially correct, explanations instead cite the power of groupthink , misplaced faith in technology , patriarchy (all of the tatzpitaniyot —or Israeli military spotters stationed around Gaza’s borders—are women), and cynicism. * How to Fix Our Global Malaise
  Foreign Policy | 05 Oct 08:00 UTC
  Focusing on local communities can have a surprising international effect.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. In a landmark June speech delivered to the Economic Club of New York commemorating the 250th anniversary of the U.S. Declaration of Independence, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent talked of a new economic statecraft, rejecting the long-established U.S. commitments to free trade, the free movement of capital, and an open world economy. The speech turned 80 years of U.S. international policy on its head. The United States would now practice the “disciplined use of America’s economic power in service of our sovereignty” by weaponizing U.S. assets, including the dollar and U.S. control of the international payments system, by building national capacity and by requiring the world to accept U.S.-imposed protocols. This would, Bessent said, entrench U.S. global leadership even if it meant imposing sanctions not just on adversaries but on long-friendly countries where necessary. These allies would be subject to “non-negotiable obligations.” In a landmark June speech delivered to the Economic Club of New York commemorating the 250th anniversary of the U.S. Declaration of Independence, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent talked of a new economic statecraft, rejecting the long-established U.S. commitments to free trade, the free movement of capital, and an open world economy. The speech turned 80 years of U.S. international policy on its head. The United States would now practice the “disciplined use of America’s economic power in service of our sovereignty” by weaponizing U.S. assets, including the dollar and U.S. control of the international payments system, by building national capacity and by requiring the world to accept U.S.-imposed protocols. This would, Bessent said, entrench U.S. global leadership even if it meant imposing sanctions not just on adversaries but on long-friendly countries where necessary. These allies would be subject to “non-negotiable obligations.” The speech followed a November 2024 note by Stephen Miran, who would later become chair of U.S. President Donald Trump’s Council of Economic Advisers, that envisaged a “Mar-a-Lago Accord” with the United States restructuring the global economic order entirely in its national interests. Both signaled that the decades-old Washington Consensus no longer had the support of Washington itself. If in the past the United States acted multilaterally in a unipolar world, now it would act unilaterally—despite the fact that ours is becoming a multipolar world. 
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/05/global-malaise-international-order-economics-civic-life-hope-citizenship-community/

* OKX and NYSE Owner ICE Plan 24/7 Tokenized Stock Trading Under SEC Exemption
  Decrypt | 05 Oct 10:22 UTC [also carried by: The Block, The Defiant]
  Filed under the SEC's new Innovation Exemption, the notice lists more than 60 stocks, including Nvidia and SpaceX, paired with stablecoins.
  FULL TEXT: OKXICE, the joint venture between crypto exchange OKX and New York Stock Exchange owner Intercontinental Exchange (ICE), has notified the SEC that it plans to launch a round-the-clock trading venue for tokenized U.S. stocks, co-chair Andrew Cuomo said in a tweet Monday. The venture published a notice dated October 4 under the SEC's "Innovation Exemption," which since September 17 has allowed qualifying venues to trade tokenized stocks without registering as exchanges. OKXICE will operate 24 hours a day, seven days a week, the notice says. Today we are announcing a major step forward for OKXICE, the joint venture between @okx and Intercontinental Exchange, parent company of @NYSE : OKXICE has notified the SEC that we intend to launch our Tokenized Securities Venue (TSV) under the SEC’s new Innovation Exemption.… — Andrew Cuomo (@andrewcuomo) October 5, 2026 The SEC does not approve venues individually. A firm that meets the exemption's conditions notifies the agency and can then operate under it, subject to those conditions. Cuomo, a former New York governor, called the filing "a landmark step toward a truly global, 24/7 Wall Street." The notice lists Nvidia, Tesla, Apple, Microsoft and SpaceX, alongside crypto-linked firms Coinbase, Circle, Strategy, Robinhood, BitGo and Securitize. Each tokenized stock will trade in a pair with one of three stablecoins: USDC, USDG or Tether's USDT. In June, an OKX spokesperson told Fortune the venture would focus on tokenizing NYSE-listed equities. Nvidia, Tesla, Apple and Microsoft all list on the Nasdaq. Chipmaker Cerebras Systems has already lodged a notice of issuer objection with the venture, the filing says. The exemption lets third parties tokenize a company's shares without its involvement, but a venue must notify the issuer and give it 30 days to object . If it does, the tokens cannot trade there. Cerebras does not appear on the notice's list. Trades will run through permissioned Uniswap v4 liquidity pools on XLayer, a layer-2 blockchain, with a custom smart contract checking every transaction. Only wallets holding a non-transferable soulbound token, issued after identity, anti-money laundering and sanctions checks run by OKX's U.S. entity, can trade or supply liquidity. The notice warns that tokenized prices, set solely by the pools, may diverge from the underlying shares, particularly outside regular exchange hours. The tokens themselves come from an unaffiliated third party, described only as "the Tokenizer," 
  https://decrypt.co/380032/okx-and-nyse-owner-ice-plan-24-7-tokenized-stock-trading-under-sec-exemption

--- Editorial (34) ---

* Toadstools and Toxins
  Longreads | 05 Oct 17:07 UTC
  "When I found the mushroom Amanita muscaria being sold in candy wrappers, I uncovered legal loopholes, dangerous dosages and more."
  FULL TEXT: When Eric Leas, a public-health scientist, discovered packages of “magic mushroom” gummies for sale in a smoke shop, he wondered: Were these candies even legal? Had they been properly tested, and did anyone actually know what constituted a safe dose? This Aeon piece follows Leas down a rabbit hole as he investigates whether the Amanita muscaria products currently on store shelves are as safe as their candy-like packaging suggests. I found a product with a very high reported amount of muscimol. It was a gummy product, one in a benign-looking package that did not have childproofing or clear warnings. I began doing the calculation no one wants to do. If a young child ate the entire package, mistaking it for candy, could they die? I took the reported amount of muscimol, the toxicology estimates (the LD50), a conversion used to translate dosing between species, and the average weight of a small child. The result looked bad. Bad enough that I did not quite trust myself. So, I called a friend, Matthieu Rouffet, a medicinal chemist and the dean of science at a local college, who does toxicology calculations more often than I do. I wanted him to tell me I had made an error. Instead, he was alarmed too. “This was a story of culinary disguise, of meat concealed in dough and murder masquerading as lunch.” “In Colorado, scientists and entrepreneurs are looking to bring psychedelic mushrooms to the masses. Could their purported healing properties help me?” “New research into the role of psychedelics upends our understanding of spirituality—and with it, our vision of the cosmos.” Cheri has been an editor at Longreads since 2014.   FULL TEXT: “Nobody calls Son Heung-min a bastard” is not a threat but a statement of fact. The mood around the national soccer team ahead of the World Cup this past summer was not positive, but fans desperately hoped that Son, who had just moved to Los Angeles to play after a decade as one of the biggest and best players in the globally popular English Premier League, could make the difference. A group of Korean journalists was watching, unaware that there was a camera rolling nearby and, as often happens when Korean men of a certain age get together, the talk turned to military service. It’s a common topic, as all able-bodied males have to start their 18-21 months of service by the time they are 28, a mandatory conscription because the country is technically still at war with its hostile neighbor, North Korea. Since 1973, however, there have been potential exemptions for elite athletes and classical musicians. Any Olympic medal or gold at the Asian Games (the second-largest multisport event in the world, held once every four years) gives the winner the right to do just three weeks of basic military training, as opposed to the usual 78 or more. Son, and some of his jogging teammates, had done just that in the past, hence the disparaging remarks that were caught on camera. “Is it because he’s the captain that he runs like a platoon leader?” one journalist asked. Another male voice piped in, despite someone warning that there was a camera. “These bastards who have not even served in the army, for God’s sake. These bastards do not even know what the army is.” The broadcaster JTBC posted the video on YouTube without even realizing what it contained. It became a huge story, and the players organized a boycott of domestic media. While Son earned his exemption legitimately on the field, there is a long-standing sensitivity to the rich and famous attempting to avoid the service completely, since the average person does not have the same privileges. * The AI boom is making the world’s cheapest smartphones disappear
  Rest of World | 05 Oct 10:00 UTC
  AI data centers are driving demand for memory chips, pushing phone makers to raise prices and abandon some of their most affordable models.
  FULL TEXT: The world’s cheapest smartphones are disappearing because AI infrastructure is absorbing the components. Prices of existing smartphone models have risen by about 15% globally this year , while newly launched models are roughly 25% more expensive than last year. A global memory chip shortage, driven by demand from AI data centers, is pushing up the cost of making smartphones. Chinese smartphone makers — which have championed affordable, feature-rich devices and account for approximately 60% of all smartphones shipped globally — have “drastically reduced” their entry-level projects this year as memory-chip costs have risen, Ivan Lam, senior analyst at research firm Counterpoint, told Rest of World . They are instead prioritizing more profitable premium devices. The smartphone price increases vary sharply by region: They have risen by 21% in India, 19% across Asia-Pacific, and 18% in the Middle East and Africa. In the U.S., they have gone up by just 5%. The shift could affect more than consumers looking for cheaper phones. For people who have yet to buy their first smartphone, higher prices could make it harder to get online at all — potentially widening the digital divide. The consequences will also reach the U.S. as more essential services move online and the global supply chain for smartphones becomes more expensive. GSMA, the global mobile industry association, had earlier said nearly 800 million more people will use mobile internet by 2030. But it is concerned that rising smartphone costs would affect this projection and widen the gap of digital inequality, Claire Sibthorpe, head of digital inclusion at the group’s nonprofit foundation, told Rest of World . “We live in an increasingly digital world, and the proliferation of technologies such as AI is creating greater digital divides and inequities,” Sibthorpe said. “Stakeholders are increasingly using AI to provide access to important services, but people won’t be able to benefit without access to an internet-enabled phone and the internet.” In 2025, more than one in four smartphones shipped globally cost less than $150 . That market is now shrinking. Chinese phonemakers including Xiaomi i Xiaomi Xiaomi is a Chinese consumer electronics company that leads the country in smartphone manufacturing and sales. READ MORE , Oppo, Vivo, and Huawei i Huawei Huawei is a Chinese technology company focused on mobile phones and telecommunications, and is seen as a poster child for China’s global tech ambitions. * A life in episodes
  Aeon | 05 Oct 10:00 UTC
  For a decade, I’ve posted episodes of my memoir to Facebook. My readers correct my understanding of my past - by Thomas Söderqvist Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: When I was about 15, alone in the flat one afternoon, I sneaked into my mother’s writing desk and found a small pocket calendar from 1946 pushed to the back of a drawer. I had grown up with my mother and her parents, not knowing who my biological father was, or how I had come about; the question was simply never asked. I turned, with a kind of dread, to the entries from nine months before I was born. In mid-March there was an ordinary note – ‘Party at the Academic Association’; she was training as a laboratory technician in Lund and liked the students’ company – and on the following day, in her hand, five words: ‘God, what have I done!’ I still remember the shock of standing there with the diary, half-guilty for trespassing on her private papers, and saying aloud, over and over: ‘That’s me – that’s me. What have you done? I’m a mistake.’ For half a century, the scene stayed as it was: fixed, private, finished. Then, about a decade ago, I posted it – to several hundred old acquaintances and strangers – on Facebook. I was in doubt; I felt faint embarrassment at making it public, but this was itself part of what I wanted to examine. Why was it shameful? What did the shame tell me about myself? And would saying it out loud, to strangers, do anything to drain the memory of its sting? The answers came from my readers, and one of them showed me something I could not have seen on my own. Betty, a friend from the years we were postdocs together at Stanford, had heard me tell the story in person long ago, and she said the written version was missing something: my voice, the intonation and the look on my face. What look, I asked – had I played it for sympathy, had I ironised it? ‘Absolutely flat,’ she wrote. ‘No affect. A Max von Sydow deadpan. But yes, you ironised.’ I had not known that about myself: that I’d delivered the central wound of my origins with no expression at all and turned it into a dry joke. T hat discovery, prompted by a reader’s response, was one of the first signs that writing one’s life in public could change how memory and narrative take shape. Writing an autobiography or memoir was traditionally a solitary undertaking. Authors worked through papers, memories and past events largely on their own, occasionally consulting family, friends or colleagues. Only after years of private work would the manuscript reach the public. Readers encountered the finished account, with little sense of the revisions, doubts and shifts in understanding that had shap
  https://aeon.co/essays/what-i-have-learned-from-posting-episodes-of-my-life-on-facebook

  FULL TEXT: When we speak of the Palestinian artist Sliman Mansour, the conversation too often narrows to a single painting, his “Camel of Hardships” (1973). It was through this painting that Mansour became known in Palestine and the Arab world. “Camel of Hardships” was inspired by the porters he saw on the streets of Jerusalem, when he passed through the Old City. It depicts an old man with traditional Palestinian clothes carrying the city of Jerusalem, as a heavy load, on his shoulder. The painting’s influence exploded with the decision to reproduce it as a poster, distributing and selling it at affordable prices for people to purchase and display in their homes. Seemingly every Palestinian home displayed “Camel of Hardships” on its walls. But while many were familiar with the poster, far fewer knew the artist who created it. That iconic painting has also dominated discussions since Mansour’s death on Aug. 24, at the age of 79, overshadowing the breadth of a career spanning more than five decades. Perhaps this is what made Mansour dislike that particular painting for such a long time. For him, although it was iconic and marked a phase in which he became more aware of his identity as a Palestinian artist, it nevertheless overshadowed much of his other work, work that people did not know much about until recent years, when he became active on social media. I knew Mansour as my father’s lifelong friend from my childhood onward. He was a gentle, humble man who frequented our home as if he were a member of the family. He was courteous, with a blond beard, a commanding presence and a cheerful soul. I remember him as a regular attendee of the League of Palestinian Artists meetings held on the long glass balcony at our home on Nablus Street in al-Bireh. I remember him sitting with my father for hours in our garden beneath the walnut tree on summer evenings, discussing art, exhibitions, artists’ meetings and elections. As a child, I would overhear the heated debates that punctuated these gatherings, along with the laughter and conversations fueled by boundless passion. The meetings often left behind clouds of white smoke tinged with the sweet aroma of pipe tobacco some artists favored, and ashtrays filled with ash and cigarette butts. Fragmented regulations could make global deployments of models more costly and difficult. Incidents of AI misuse and malfunction are surfacing across Africa. Kenya’s predictive machine learning algorithm for setting health insurance contributions has driven up costs for poor households. Scammers in West Africa have used AI-generated content to blackmail victims in extortion schemes . The militant group Boko Haram has also used AI to organize attacks and design weapons after bypassing safeguards. “I think it’s going to take a potentially catastrophic incident for governments to really take [heed],” Shock said, which he dubbed his optimistic view. “The pessimistic view is we don’t stop it in time.” Africa currently lags behind other regions in AI adoption, though its companies are moving quickly. Out of 85 large companies surveyed by PricewaterhouseCoopers this year, more than 80% were running AI pilots.   FULL TEXT: Chicago, Illinois, US, 25 December 2021. Photo by Cheney Orr/Reuters The day before we spoke, King 1 , 26, had learned that his friend Lamont had been killed. He wanted the people who killed Lamont to feel what he was feeling. And if he couldn’t find them, he’d find someone else he had beef with. The back roads of southwest Virginia wind, crest, and plunge through lush valleys of oak, poplar, birch, and maple trees. It’s easy to lose track of time and distance there. People drive slower. Cows graze in pastures. And there are no placards festooned with Motel 6 and Starbucks logos, flattening the American landscape into a homogeneous blur. Any signs you might see are low to the ground and handmade, protesting a data center, for instance, or thanking Jesus. I’d gone to this rural pocket of the country for the 90th annual Old Fiddler’s Convention in Galax, an independent city of just under 7,000 near the North Carolina border whose population, for six days in early August, balloons to around four times that. They arrive in RVs and Casitas, camper vans and cars to stake out 20-by-35-foot plots in Felts Park, a 28-acre fairground in the heart of Galax, transforming it into a massive, muddy parking lot sprinkled with portable toilets and a cluster of food trucks, offering loaded potatoes, funnel cake, and blooming onions. The Old Fiddler’s Convention—or just Galax, as the diehards call it—is the oldest and largest fiddlers’ convention in the world, a place where banjo pickers, guitar players, and other maestros of old-time strings gather to see how they stack up. Founded in 1935, Galax has been canceled only twice since then: once during World War II, due to travel restrictions, and again in 2020, due to the COVID-19 pandemic. Many Galaxers have been going every year for decades, some for as long as they’ve been alive. There are scads of rules: If you play clawhammer-style banjo in the bluegrass band competition, you will get disqualified. Same if you play bluegrass-style banjo in the old-time competition. If you break a string while performing, you must stop immediately. Playing time during competition is limited to two minutes and 30 seconds. And, crucially, no electric instruments of any kind are allowed, either on stage or in the tightly packed fairgrounds, where self-described hillbillies, accountants, firefighters, and session musicians pick day and night in blistering, impromptu jams amid the RVs. * Reason is more than a tool
  Aeon | 01 Oct 10:00 UTC
  If intelligence is merely optimisation then machines will outrun us. Kant tells us why human reason is so much more - by Sasha Mudd Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Illustration by Aeon/Daniel Ceng/Anadolu/Getty Images A few months ago, I sat in a packed university auditorium listening to a celebrated computer scientist explain the brilliance of large language models (LLMs). These systems optimise, he said. And they improve at optimisation on the basis of past experience. He paused. That, he said with a smile, is intelligence. The audience nodded along approvingly. Meanwhile, a philosophical alarm bell began sounding in my head. Not because the speaker’s claim was implausible. On the contrary, as a description of much contemporary artificial intelligence, it seemed apt. AI systems are built around objectives, losses, rewards, predictions, preferences, benchmarks and feedback loops. They are trained to perform better relative to some standard of success. In one form or another, they optimise. That is a great part of their technical power. What troubled me was the ease with which a definition appropriate to machines sounded like a definition of intelligence as such. That slide from engineering model to human anthropology is easy to miss. We begin by saying: this is how machines work. Then, almost without noticing, we say: this is what intelligence is. And then, more dangerously still: perhaps this is what we are. An old argument about human beings had entered the room without announcing itself. It sounded, instead, like a definition. The AI revolution has brought one of the Enlightenment’s deepest quarrels back to life – and made one side of it seem like plain common sense. In my view, the wrong side. Hence the alarm bell. I n the 18th century, philosophers were concerned not only with the question of what reason is, but with what it is good for. Is reason essentially a servant, a clever instrument for achieving ends supplied from elsewhere? Or is it sovereign, capable of judging, revising and even legislating ends for itself? Can it ultimately shape the direction of human life, or is it merely along for the ride? Everything – from science to social progress to the viability of the French Revolution – seemed to hang in the balance. David Hume , the cool-eyed Scottish anatomist of human nature, gave the most famous answer on one side: ‘Reason is, and ought only to be the slave of the passions,’ he wrote in A Treatise of Human Nature (1739). It can ‘never pretend to any other office than to serve and obey them.’ Reason does not command. It defers. It does not tell us what to want or what might be worth wanting. Instead, i
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-real-intelligence-is-something-more-than-optimisation

It was in this borderland, in the village of Tsagan-Chelutai — 130 miles from the junction between Russia, Mongolia and China — that Rabdanov was born in 1994. The main street leading to my place wasn’t well lit, with dark stretches between streetlamps. There was a huddle of men under one, some standing around, some lounging against the post. They moved in the orange light, turning as one to face me. Something shifted in the air. An earlier version of me would have been slower to respond, given them the benefit of the doubt. I doubled back, cut across fences, and scaled half walls. I made no sound at all; I made it home. I had made a spiraling maze in real time. They could not find me. In his essay “ From the Tree to the Labyrinth ,” Umberto Eco, a lover of labyrinths, explores how we “create and organize knowledge.” Moving away from the restrictive “tree” model, Eco approaches the “labyrinth” model, listing three kinds—a unicursal labyrinth, with only one path to the center and back; a multicursal maze, with many paths, all but one leading to dead ends; and an infinite network of interlinked points, which he calls by the borrowed term “rhizome.” There are many labyrinths and mazes woven into my memory—verbal, textual, graphical, physical, metaphorical. Tamil kolam s , for instance: My mother draws small, perfect threshold patterns at home, and immense, geometrically flawless labyrinths and mazes for events—no outlines, no tools, just her seeing eye and moving hand. When the sky is still dark, she dips folded muslin in wet, ground rice and draws liquid lines on the clean floor. She must draw each bit before the white dries on her hand, which it does almost instantly. Nothing can be erased or redrawn. She must keep moving until the whole kolam is done—the body walking the labyrinth even as it summons it out of nothing, the labyrinth making the world even as it is devoured by little black ants. Eco’s rhizome model, to its credit, says we may never know everything, but we can keep going. Jorge Luis Borges certainly did, over and over like a modern-day Scheherazade. * Don’t use the ‘C-word’
  Aeon | 29 Sep 10:00 UTC
  A cancer diagnosis carries with it fear and upheaval. For many patients the cellular changes do not warrant the label - by Matthew R Cooperberg Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Light micrograph of a section through a prostate gland with benign prostatic hyperplasia. Photo by Steve Gschmeissner/SPL William came to me for a third opinion. He was 53 and healthy, with a wife and two teenage children, a busy job, and a typically hectic work-and-family schedule. He’d taken time to do the right thing and had visited his primary care provider for a routine health exam and blood tests, his first in many years. A few weeks later, he’d found himself in an unfamiliar urologist’s office, at the edge of an abyss, ears ringing with what may be three of the most dreaded words in medicine: ‘You have cancer.’ This diagnosis portends pain, suffering and death: in the United States alone, cancer kills more than 600,000 people a year, enough lives to empty a city. Yet, receiving a cancer diagnosis doesn’t necessarily mean what it once did. William was in very broad company: more than 2 million Americans a year receive the diagnosis and, for many, the finding entails little to no threat to length or quality of life, except through the ill effects of treatments. However, patients labelled with the ‘C-word’ face substantial clinical, psychological, social and economic consequences. This disconnect – between what the word evokes in my exam room and what it often describes in contemporary practice – drives controversies and variability in screening practices, and in light of our growing understanding of cellular biology, demands that we rethink what we really mean when we apply the label ‘cancer’. William’s cancer was in his prostate, an organ of whose existence he was previously only vaguely aware, but whose removal or radiation risked impairments to urinary, bowel and sexual function. His biopsy had revealed a prostate tumour we designate ‘grade group 1’, which features few molecular hallmarks of cancer and no immediate potential for metastasis or death. Nonetheless, beset with many competing opinions from his rapidly expanding medical team, William found himself rushing headlong toward treatment, and wrestling with a constant anxiety he’d never felt before. By the time he reached my office, he had taken medical leave from work and confided that, perversely, he was smoking for the first time in decades. W hen I started medical school in the 1990s, I found cancer fascinating and terrifying exactly because of the betrayal it represented.
